I came in because I just… I can’t shake this heavy feeling. It’s like this hopelessness has settled over everything, and I keep trying to tell myself to get up, to handle things, to be practical about it, but I can’t seem to move out from under it.

After the divorce, I really tried to rebuild my life, you know? I thought I was putting pieces back together—work, routines, just trying to make something solid again—and now it feels like all of that has collapsed. Like everything I built is just sitting on top of me, and I’m stuck underneath it. I don’t really know what else to do, so… that’s why I’m here.

About a month, I think. It didn’t happen all at once, exactly, but it started really settling in around when the financial mess after the divorce became… unavoidable, I guess. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m… not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I’m not myself, but when you say “enjoying things,” it’s hard for me to separate what I actually don’t enjoy anymore from what I just haven’t really had the energy or space to even think about.

Before all this, I had things that felt normal or comforting, but lately everything is sort of filtered through this heaviness, if that makes sense. I’ll try to do something familiar and I’m half there, half thinking about everything that’s wrong, so it’s difficult to tell whether the thing itself doesn’t feel good anymore or whether I’m just… buried under everything.

So I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed clear enjoyment the way I used to, but I also don’t know if I can give you a clean yes or no. Everything just feels kind of muted right now.

But it’s been hanging over me for weeks now—about a month—and it hasn’t really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that. I don’t know that I can point to anything and say, yes, that still gives me real pleasure. That’s part of what feels so unsettling. I’ll do things sometimes because I know they’re things I used to like, or because they’re supposed to help, or because I’m trying not to just sit there with the heaviness, but it doesn’t really land the same way.

It’s more like… I might have a moment where something is distracting, maybe, or familiar, but I wouldn’t call it pleasure exactly. It’s like the volume has been turned way down on everything. And then I start questioning myself—am I not enjoying it, or am I just so preoccupied that I can’t access that feeling? I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed anything recently that really cuts through it.

I’ll try to do something familiar and I’m half there, half thinking about everything that’s wrong, so it’s difficult to tell whether the thing itself doesn’t feel good anymore or whether I’m just… buried under everything.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed clear enjoyment the way I used to, but I also don’t know if I can give you a clean yes or no. Everything just feels kind of muted right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to anything and say, yes, that still gives me real pleasure. That’s part of what feels so unsettling. I’ll do things sometimes because I know they’re things I used to like, or because they’re supposed to help, or because I’m trying not to just sit there with the heaviness, but it doesn’t really land the same way.\n\nIt’s more like… I might have a moment where something is distracting, maybe, or familiar, but I wouldn’t call it pleasure exactly. It’s like the volume has been turned way down on everything. And then I start questioning myself—am I not enjoying it, or am I just so preoccupied that I can’t access that feeling? I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed anything recently that really cuts through it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had any thoughts about harming yourself or wishing you weren't alive since this heavy feeling started?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / civil_engineer / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Death_Suicide\nmanifestation: \"I’ve been thinking that it would be easier if I didn’t wake up, and the thoughts got intense enough that I scared myself and called the employee assistance program.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I… I have. It’s hard to say out loud, because I don’t think of myself as someone who would say that, but since this started I’ve had thoughts like… it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Not exactly like I’m calmly deciding something, more like I’m so tired and everything feels so impossible that not having to face another day starts to sound like relief.

And I tried to tell myself it was just a passing thought, like people say things when they’re overwhelmed, but it got stronger than that. There was a point where it scared me because it didn’t feel like just some distant, dramatic thought anymore—it felt too close, like I couldn’t trust where my mind was going. So I called the employee assistance program through work. I didn’t really know what else to do, and I was embarrassed, honestly, but I scared myself enough that I made the call.

  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been tracking it closely, and I don’t think I’ve noticed some obvious dramatic change where my clothes suddenly fit completely differently or anything like that.

It’s possible there’s been some change, but I can’t confidently say. I’ve been so focused on just getting through the day that I haven’t been paying much attention to the scale or even to my body in that way. I guess if you’re asking whether I’ve clearly lost or gained a lot, I don’t think I can say that I have. I just don’t know for sure.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely. Sleep has been… really bad. It’s not just that I’m going to bed late or something normal like that. I’ll be exhausted, like my body feels heavy and I know I should be able to sleep, but then I just lie there staring at the ceiling.

My mind starts replaying everything — bills, things I’m behind on, mistakes I might have made at work, things I said wrong or forgot to do — and it just loops. I’ll think I’ve settled down, and then another thought comes in and I’m wide awake again.

And then other nights, or sometimes the same night, I’ll wake up before dawn and it feels like I never really slept at all. Like I must have been asleep at some point, but I don’t feel rested. I just wake up already tense and tired, and then I’m stuck there waiting for the day to start.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Very much. Everything feels slowed down, like I’m trying to move through something thick. Getting through a normal workday feels like dragging wet concrete. I know that sounds dramatic, but that’s honestly the closest way I can describe it.

Even things that should be automatic feel like they require this huge negotiation with myself. Taking a shower, getting dressed, opening my laptop… it’s like I look at the thing and I understand what needs to happen, but my body just doesn’t want to start. Or it starts, but every step feels heavier than it should.

At work especially, I’ll sit there knowing I need to answer an email or pull up a file, and it feels almost ridiculous how much effort it takes just to begin. It’s not that I don’t care. I do care, and that almost makes it worse, because I can see the work piling up and I’m just… not able to move through it the way I normally would.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just… I can’t shake this heavy feeling. It’s like this hopelessness has settled over everything, and I keep trying to tell myself to get up, to handle things, to be practical about it, but I can’t seem to move out from under it.\n\nAfter the divorce, I really tried to rebuild my life, you know? I thought I was putting pieces back together—work, routines, just trying to make something solid again—and now it feels like all of that has collapsed. Like everything I built is just sitting on top of me, and I’m stuck underneath it. I don’t really know what else to do, so… that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, hopeless feeling?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It didn’t happen all at once, exactly, but it started really settling in around when the financial mess after the divorce became… unavoidable, I guess. Like the paperwork and bills and all the practical things I thought I could manage started piling up, and then this heaviness just kind of came down over everything.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was just stress, that anyone would feel overwhelmed, and maybe if I got organized or made a plan it would ease up. But it’s been hanging over me for weeks now—about a month—and it hasn’t really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I’m not myself, but when you say “enjoying things,” it’s hard for me to separate what I actually don’t enjoy anymore from what I just haven’t really had the energy or space to even think about.\n\nBefore all this, I had things that felt normal or comforting, but lately everything is sort of filtered through this heaviness, if that makes sense. I’ll try to do something familiar and I’m half there, half thinking about everything that’s wrong, so it’s difficult to tell whether the thing itself doesn’t feel good anymore or whether I’m just… buried under everything.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed clear enjoyment the way I used to, but I also don’t know if I can give you a clean yes or no. Everything just feels kind of muted right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to anything and say, yes, that still gives me real pleasure. That’s part of what feels so unsettling. I’ll do things sometimes because I know they’re things I used to like, or because they’re supposed to help, or because I’m trying not to just sit there with the heaviness, but it doesn’t really land the same way.\n\nIt’s more like… I might have a moment where something is distracting, maybe, or familiar, but I wouldn’t call it pleasure exactly. It’s like the volume has been turned way down on everything. And then I start questioning myself—am I not enjoying it, or am I just so preoccupied that I can’t access that feeling? I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed anything recently that really cuts through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about harming yourself or wishing you weren't alive since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes. I… I have. It’s hard to say out loud, because I don’t think of myself as someone who would say that, but since this started I’ve had thoughts like… it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Not exactly like I’m calmly deciding something, more like I’m so tired and everything feels so impossible that not having to face another day starts to sound like relief.\n\nAnd I tried to tell myself it was just a passing thought, like people say things when they’re overwhelmed, but it got stronger than that. There was a point where it scared me because it didn’t feel like just some distant, dramatic thought anymore—it felt too close, like I couldn’t trust where my mind was going. So I called the employee assistance program through work. I didn’t really know what else to do, and I was embarrassed, honestly, but I scared myself enough that I made the call.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been tracking it closely, and I don’t think I’ve noticed some obvious dramatic change where my clothes suddenly fit completely differently or anything like that.\n\nIt’s possible there’s been some change, but I can’t confidently say. I’ve been so focused on just getting through the day that I haven’t been paying much attention to the scale or even to my body in that way. I guess if you’re asking whether I’ve clearly lost or gained a lot, I don’t think I can say that I have. I just don’t know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Sleep has been… really bad. It’s not just that I’m going to bed late or something normal like that. I’ll be exhausted, like my body feels heavy and I know I should be able to sleep, but then I just lie there staring at the ceiling.\n\nMy mind starts replaying everything — bills, things I’m behind on, mistakes I might have made at work, things I said wrong or forgot to do — and it just loops. I’ll think I’ve settled down, and then another thought comes in and I’m wide awake again.\n\nAnd then other nights, or sometimes the same night, I’ll wake up before dawn and it feels like I never really slept at all. Like I must have been asleep at some point, but I don’t feel rested. I just wake up already tense and tired, and then I’m stuck there waiting for the day to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. Everything feels slowed down, like I’m trying to move through something thick. Getting through a normal workday feels like dragging wet concrete. I know that sounds dramatic, but that’s honestly the closest way I can describe it.\n\nEven things that should be automatic feel like they require this huge negotiation with myself. Taking a shower, getting dressed, opening my laptop… it’s like I look at the thing and I understand what needs to happen, but my body just doesn’t want to start. Or it starts, but every step feels heavier than it should.\n\nAt work especially, I’ll sit there knowing I need to answer an email or pull up a file, and it feels almost ridiculous how much effort it takes just to begin. It’s not that I don’t care. I do care, and that almost makes it worse, because I can see the work piling up and I’m just… not able to move through it the way I normally would.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Yes, but it’s a little confusing because at the same time I feel slowed down and heavy. It’s like physically I’m exhausted, but I also can’t just sit still with it.

I’ve been pacing around my apartment a lot, especially when there’s nothing useful left to do. I’ll walk from the kitchen to the living room and back again, checking my phone, opening my laptop, closing it, standing up again. It doesn’t even feel productive half the time, just this internal pressure like I should be doing something.

And at work, or around work, I started pushing myself into extra things — like extra proposals, calling colleagues because I suddenly had another idea, volunteering for tasks that no one actually asked me to take on. I’d tell myself, “If I just take care of this one more thing, maybe I’ll feel less awful,” but then it would turn into five things.

So yes, restless is probably the right word. Not always fidgety in a small way, but more like I can’t settle, and I keep trying to force myself into activity even when I don’t have the energy for it.

Or it starts, but every step feels heavier than it should.\n\nAt work especially, I’ll sit there knowing I need to answer an email or pull up a file, and it feels almost ridiculous how much effort it takes just to begin. It’s not that I don’t care. I do care, and that almost makes it worse, because I can see the work piling up and I’m just… not able to move through it the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes, but it’s a little confusing because at the same time I feel slowed down and heavy. It’s like physically I’m exhausted, but I also can’t just sit still with it.\n\nI’ve been pacing around my apartment a lot, especially when there’s nothing useful left to do. I’ll walk from the kitchen to the living room and back again, checking my phone, opening my laptop, closing it, standing up again. It doesn’t even feel productive half the time, just this internal pressure like I should be doing something.\n\nAnd at work, or around work, I started pushing myself into extra things — like extra proposals, calling colleagues because I suddenly had another idea, volunteering for tasks that no one actually asked me to take on. I’d tell myself, “If I just take care of this one more thing, maybe I’ll feel less awful,” but then it would turn into five things.\n\nSo yes, restless is probably the right word. Not always fidgety in a small way, but more like I can’t settle, and I keep trying to force myself into activity even when I don’t have the energy for it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you since this heavy feeling started?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / civil_engineer / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Worthlessness_Guilt\nmanifestation: \"I keep telling myself I’m a terrible engineer, a failed wife, and a burden, and I replay every missed calculation or tense email like proof that I ruin things.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Constantly. And it’s not even proportional to what actually happened, I know that somewhere in my head, but it doesn’t feel that way.

I keep telling myself I’m a terrible engineer. Like if I miss something small, or I have to double-check a calculation, or someone sends back a comment on a plan, my brain turns it into this whole indictment of me as a person. I’ll replay it over and over, like, “How could you miss that? What if that caused a bigger problem? What if everyone knows you’re barely holding it together?” Even tense emails — not even openly hostile ones, just slightly curt or corrective — I’ll read them again and again and think, “See, you’re ruining things. You’re making everyone else’s job harder.”

And it’s not just work. I keep thinking I’m a failed wife, like I should be better, warmer, more present, less exhausting to be around. I feel like a burden. Like people have to manage me, or make allowances for me, or tiptoe around me, and then I feel guilty for that too.

It’s like every little mistake becomes proof of some bigger failure. A missed calculation, a delayed response, a tense conversation — I collect them in my head and use them against myself. I don’t want to, but I do.

But it’s been hanging over me for weeks now—about a month—and it hasn’t really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I’m not myself, but when you say “enjoying things,” it’s hard for me to separate what I actually don’t enjoy anymore from what I just haven’t really had the energy or space to even think about.\n\nBefore all this, I had things that felt normal or comforting, but lately everything is sort of filtered through this heaviness, if that makes sense. I’ll try to do something familiar and I’m half there, half thinking about everything that’s wrong, so it’s difficult to tell whether the thing itself doesn’t feel good anymore or whether I’m just… buried under everything.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed clear enjoyment the way I used to, but I also don’t know if I can give you a clean yes or no. Everything just feels kind of muted right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to anything and say, yes, that still gives me real pleasure. That’s part of what feels so unsettling. I’ll think I’ve settled down, and then another thought comes in and I’m wide awake again.\n\nAnd then other nights, or sometimes the same night, I’ll wake up before dawn and it feels like I never really slept at all. Like I must have been asleep at some point, but I don’t feel rested. nothing useful left to do.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I was making questionable calls at work and falling apart in meetings, and my supervisor had to take me off active project decisions. At home I stopped keeping up with basic routines and ended up being sent to the emergency department because I didn’t feel safe with my thoughts.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 weeks\n  manifestation: The out-of-control shift from being intensely wired to crashing has been going on for about two weeks.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 month\n  manifestation: The depression has been hanging over me for about a month, starting around the financial mess after the divorce.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / civil_engineer / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S024\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. Everything feels slowed down, like I’m trying to move through something thick. Getting through a normal workday feels like dragging wet concrete. I know that sounds dramatic, but that’s honestly the closest way I can describe it.\n\nEven things that should be automatic feel like they require this huge negotiation with myself. Taking a shower, getting dressed, opening my laptop… it’s like I look at the thing and I understand what needs to happen, but my body just doesn’t want to start. Or it starts, but every step feels heavier than it should.\n\nAt work especially, I’ll sit there knowing I need to answer an email or pull up a file, and it feels almost ridiculous how much effort it takes just to begin. It’s not that I don’t care. I do care, and that almost makes it worse, because I can see the work piling up and I’m just… not able to move through it the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes, but it’s a little confusing because at the same time I feel slowed down and heavy. It’s like physically I’m exhausted, but I also can’t just sit still with it.\n\nI’ve been pacing around my apartment a lot, especially when there’s nothing useful left to do. I’ll walk from the kitchen to the living room and back again, checking my phone, opening my laptop, closing it, standing up again. It doesn’t even feel productive half the time, just this internal pressure like I should be doing something.\n\nAnd at work, or around work, I started pushing myself into extra things — like extra proposals, calling colleagues because I suddenly had another idea, volunteering for tasks that no one actually asked me to take on. I’d tell myself, “If I just take care of this one more thing, maybe I’ll feel less awful,” but then it would turn into five things.\n\nSo yes, restless is probably the right word. Not always fidgety in a small way, but more like I can’t settle, and I keep trying to force myself into activity even when I don’t have the energy for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes. Constantly. And it’s not even proportional to what actually happened, I know that somewhere in my head, but it doesn’t feel that way.\n\nI keep telling myself I’m a terrible engineer. Like if I miss something small, or I have to double-check a calculation, or someone sends back a comment on a plan, my brain turns it into this whole indictment of me as a person. I’ll replay it over and over, like, “How could you miss that? What if that caused a bigger problem? What if everyone knows you’re barely holding it together?” Even tense emails — not even openly hostile ones, just slightly curt or corrective — I’ll read them again and again and think, “See, you’re ruining things. You’re making everyone else’s job harder.”\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I keep thinking I’m a failed wife, like I should be better, warmer, more present, less exhausting to be around. I feel like a burden. Like people have to manage me, or make allowances for me, or tiptoe around me, and then I feel guilty for that too.\n\nIt’s like every little mistake becomes proof of some bigger failure. A missed calculation, a delayed response, a tense conversation — I collect them in my head and use them against myself. I don’t want to, but I do.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt like you're moving or reacting more slowly than usual since this heavy feeling started?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean, it feels less like I’m choosing to move slowly and more like everything takes this unreasonable amount of effort now.

Getting through a normal workday feels like dragging wet concrete. I’ll know exactly what I need to do, but getting myself to actually do it physically — opening the laptop, starting a task, even just shifting from one thing to the next — feels like I’m trying to push through something heavy. It’s embarrassing because these are basic things, things I normally don’t even think about.

Even taking a shower can feel like this whole project. Not emotionally in some dramatic way, just… the energy required to stand up, get the towel, turn on the water, wash my hair — it feels like more than I have available. And then I get frustrated with myself because I’m like, “Why is this so hard? It’s just a shower.”

So yes, I do feel slower. My reactions, my movements, my ability to get started — all of it feels delayed, like my body is lagging behind what my mind is telling it to do.

Like everything I built is just sitting on top of me, and I’m stuck underneath it. I don’t really know what else to do, so… that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, hopeless feeling?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It didn’t happen all at once, exactly, but it started really settling in around when the financial mess after the divorce became… unavoidable, I guess. Like the paperwork and bills and all the practical things I thought I could manage started piling up, and then this heaviness just kind of came down over everything.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was just stress, that anyone would feel overwhelmed, and maybe if I got organized or made a plan it would ease up. But it’s been hanging over me for weeks now—about a month—and it hasn’t really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I’m not myself, but when you say “enjoying things,” it’s hard for me to separate what I actually don’t enjoy anymore from what I just haven’t really had the energy or space to even think about.\n\nBefore all this, I had things that felt normal or comforting, but lately everything is sort of filtered through this heaviness, if that makes sense. I’ll try to do something familiar and I’m half there, half thinking about everything that’s wrong, so it’s difficult to tell whether the thing itself doesn’t feel good anymore or whether I’m just… buried under everything.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed clear enjoyment the way I used to, but I also don’t know if I can give you a clean yes or no. Everything just feels kind of muted right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to anything and say, yes, that still gives me real pleasure. That’s part of what feels so unsettling. I’ll do things sometimes because I know they’re things I used to like, or because they’re supposed to help, or because I’m trying not to just sit there with the heaviness, but it doesn’t really land the same way.\n\nIt’s more like… I might have a moment where something is distracting, maybe, or familiar, but I wouldn’t call it pleasure exactly. It’s like the volume has been turned way down on everything. And then I start questioning myself—am I not enjoying it, or am I just so preoccupied that I can’t access that feeling? I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed anything recently that really cuts through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about harming yourself or wishing you weren't alive since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes. I… I have. It’s hard to say out loud, because I don’t think of myself as someone who would say that, but since this started I’ve had thoughts like… it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Not exactly like I’m calmly deciding something, more like I’m so tired and everything feels so impossible that not having to face another day starts to sound like relief.\n\nAnd I tried to tell myself it was just a passing thought, like people say things when they’re overwhelmed, but it got stronger than that. There was a point where it scared me because it didn’t feel like just some distant, dramatic thought anymore—it felt too close, like I couldn’t trust where my mind was going. So I called the employee assistance program through work. I didn’t really know what else to do, and I was embarrassed, honestly, but I scared myself enough that I made the call.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been tracking it closely, and I don’t think I’ve noticed some obvious dramatic change where my clothes suddenly fit completely differently or anything like that.\n\nIt’s possible there’s been some change, but I can’t confidently say. I’ve been so focused on just getting through the day that I haven’t been paying much attention to the scale or even to my body in that way. I guess if you’re asking whether I’ve clearly lost or gained a lot, I don’t think I can say that I have. I just don’t know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Sleep has been… really bad. It’s not just that I’m going to bed late or something normal like that. I’ll be exhausted, like my body feels heavy and I know I should be able to sleep, but then I just lie there staring at the ceiling.\n\nMy mind starts replaying everything — bills, things I’m behind on, mistakes I might have made at work, things I said wrong or forgot to do — and it just loops. I’ll think I’ve settled down, and then another thought comes in and I’m wide awake again.\n\nAnd then other nights, or sometimes the same night, I’ll wake up before dawn and it feels like I never really slept at all. Like I must have been asleep at some point, but I don’t feel rested. I just wake up already tense and tired, and then I’m stuck there waiting for the day to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. Everything feels slowed down, like I’m trying to move through something thick. Getting through a normal workday feels like dragging wet concrete. I know that sounds dramatic, but that’s honestly the closest way I can describe it.\n\nEven things that should be automatic feel like they require this huge negotiation with myself. Taking a shower, getting dressed, opening my laptop… it’s like I look at the thing and I understand what needs to happen, but my body just doesn’t want to start. I do care, and that almost makes it worse, because I can see the work piling up and I’m just… not able to move through it the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes, but it’s a little confusing because at the same time I feel slowed down and heavy. I’ll think I’ve settled down, and then another thought comes in and I’m wide awake again.\n\nAnd then other nights, or sometimes the same night, I’ll wake up before dawn and it feels like I never really slept at all. Like I must have been asleep at some point, but I don’t feel rested. I just wake up already tense and tired, and then I’m stuck there waiting for the day to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. Everything feels slowed down, like I’m trying to move through something thick. Getting through a normal workday feels like dragging wet concrete. I know that sounds dramatic, but that’s honestly the closest way I can describe it.\n\nEven things that should be automatic feel like they require this huge negotiation with myself. Taking a shower, getting dressed, opening my laptop… it’s like I look at the thing and I understand what needs to happen, but my body just doesn’t want to start. Or it starts, but every step feels heavier than it should.\n\nAt work especially, I’ll sit there knowing I need to answer an email or pull up a file, and it feels almost ridiculous how much effort it takes just to begin. It’s not that I don’t care. I do care, and that almost makes it worse, because I can see the work piling up and I’m just… not able to move through it the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes, but it’s a little confusing because at the same time I feel slowed down and heavy. It’s like physically I’m exhausted, but I also can’t just sit still with it.\n\nI’ve been pacing around my apartment a lot, especially when there’s nothing useful left to do. I’ll walk from the kitchen to the living room and back again, checking my phone, opening my laptop, closing it, standing up again. It doesn’t even feel productive half the time, just this internal pressure like I should be doing something.\n\nAnd at work, or around work, I started pushing myself into extra things — like extra proposals, calling colleagues because I suddenly had another idea, volunteering for tasks that no one actually asked me to take on. I’d tell myself, “If I just take care of this one more thing, maybe I’ll feel less awful,” but then it would turn into five things.\n\nSo yes, restless is probably the right word. Not always fidgety in a small way, but more like I can’t settle, and I keep trying to force myself into activity even when I don’t have the energy for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes. Constantly. And it’s not even proportional to what actually happened, I know that somewhere in my head, but it doesn’t feel that way.\n\nI keep telling myself I’m a terrible engineer. Like if I miss something small, or I have to double-check a calculation, or someone sends back a comment on a plan, my brain turns it into this whole indictment of me as a person. I’ll replay it over and over, like, “How could you miss that? What if that caused a bigger problem? What if everyone knows you’re barely holding it together?” Even tense emails — not even openly hostile ones, just slightly curt or corrective — I’ll read them again and again and think, “See, you’re ruining things. You’re making everyone else’s job harder.”\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I keep thinking I’m a failed wife, like I should be better, warmer, more present, less exhausting to be around. I feel like a burden. Like people have to manage me, or make allowances for me, or tiptoe around me, and then I feel guilty for that too.\n\nIt’s like every little mistake becomes proof of some bigger failure. A missed calculation, a delayed response, a tense conversation — I collect them in my head and use them against myself. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean… yes, definitely, though it’s hard to separate it from everything else because before I got to this heavy crash, I was kind of all over the place emotionally.

There were times when I felt almost strangely wired, like everything was finally clicking and I could see exactly what needed to happen, and I was talking faster and pushing things forward because it all felt so obvious to me. And then if someone in a meeting slowed it down or questioned a detail, I would get irritated really fast. Not just mildly annoyed — I’d snap. Over things that, normally, I think I would have just discussed or let go.

Like someone would make a small comment on a design approach or ask for clarification, and I’d hear it as them being obstructive or missing the point, and I’d respond sharply. Then later I’d think, “Why did I come at them like that?” because the comment itself wasn’t that big of a deal. It was like I had no buffer. Everything felt urgent, and anyone not moving at the same pace felt like they were in the way.

So yes, intense irritability happened. It wasn’t constant every second, but when it came up it was much stronger than my usual reaction, and it came out at people in meetings over really small things.

I know that sounds dramatic, but that’s honestly the closest way I can describe it.\n\nEven things that should be automatic feel like they require this huge negotiation with myself. Taking a shower, getting dressed, opening my laptop… it’s like I look at the thing and I understand what needs to happen, but my body just doesn’t want to start. Or it starts, but every step feels heavier than it should.\n\nAt work especially, I’ll sit there knowing I need to answer an email or pull up a file, and it feels almost ridiculous how much effort it takes just to begin. It’s not that I don’t care. I do care, and that almost makes it worse, because I can see the work piling up and I’m just… not able to move through it the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes, but it’s a little confusing because at the same time I feel slowed down and heavy. It’s like physically I’m exhausted, but I also can’t just sit still with it.\n\nI’ve been pacing around my apartment a lot, especially when there’s nothing useful left to do. I’ll walk from the kitchen to the living room and back again, checking my phone, opening my laptop, closing it, standing up again. It doesn’t even feel productive half the time, just this internal pressure like I should be doing something.\n\nAnd at work, or around work, I started pushing myself into extra things — like extra proposals, calling colleagues because I suddenly had another idea, volunteering for tasks that no one actually asked me to take on. I’d tell myself, “If I just take care of this one more thing, maybe I’ll feel less awful,” but then it would turn into five things.\n\nSo yes, restless is probably the right word. Not always fidgety in a small way, but more like I can’t settle, and I keep trying to force myself into activity even when I don’t have the energy for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes. Constantly. And it’s not even proportional to what actually happened, I know that somewhere in my head, but it doesn’t feel that way.\n\nI keep telling myself I’m a terrible engineer. Like if I miss something small, or I have to double-check a calculation, or someone sends back a comment on a plan, my brain turns it into this whole indictment of me as a person. I’ll replay it over and over, like, “How could you miss that? What if that caused a bigger problem? What if everyone knows you’re barely holding it together?” Even tense emails — not even openly hostile ones, just slightly curt or corrective — I’ll read them again and again and think, “See, you’re ruining things. You’re making everyone else’s job harder.”\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I keep thinking I’m a failed wife, like I should be better, warmer, more present, less exhausting to be around. I feel like a burden. Like people have to manage me, or make allowances for me, or tiptoe around me, and then I feel guilty for that too.\n\nIt’s like every little mistake becomes proof of some bigger failure. A missed calculation, a delayed response, a tense conversation — I collect them in my head and use them against myself. I don’t want to, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving or reacting more slowly than usual since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, it feels less like I’m choosing to move slowly and more like everything takes this unreasonable amount of effort now.\n\nGetting through a normal workday feels like dragging wet concrete. I’ll know exactly what I need to do, but getting myself to actually do it physically — opening the laptop, starting a task, even just shifting from one thing to the next — feels like I’m trying to push through something heavy. It’s embarrassing because these are basic things, things I normally don’t even think about.\n\nEven taking a shower can feel like this whole project. Not emotionally in some dramatic way, just… the energy required to stand up, get the towel, turn on the water, wash my hair — it feels like more than I have available. And then I get frustrated with myself because I’m like, “Why is this so hard? It’s just a shower.”\n\nSo yes, I do feel slower. My reactions, my movements, my ability to get started — all of it feels delayed, like my body is lagging behind what my mind is telling it to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense irritability since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, definitely, though it’s hard to separate it from everything else because before I got to this heavy crash, I was kind of all over the place emotionally.\n\nThere were times when I felt almost strangely wired, like everything was finally clicking and I could see exactly what needed to happen, and I was talking faster and pushing things forward because it all felt so obvious to me. And then if someone in a meeting slowed it down or questioned a detail, I would get irritated really fast. Not just mildly annoyed — I’d snap. Over things that, normally, I think I would have just discussed or let go.\n\nLike someone would make a small comment on a design approach or ask for clarification, and I’d hear it as them being obstructive or missing the point, and I’d respond sharply. Then later I’d think, “Why did I come at them like that?” because the comment itself wasn’t that big of a deal. It was like I had no buffer. I don’t want to, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving or reacting more slowly than usual since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, it feels less like I’m choosing to move slowly and more like everything takes this unreasonable amount of effort now.\n\nGetting through a normal workday feels like dragging wet concrete. I’ll know exactly what I need to do, but getting myself to actually do it physically — opening the laptop, starting a task, even just shifting from one thing to the next — feels like I’m trying to push through something heavy. It’s embarrassing because these are basic things, things I normally don’t even think about.\n\nEven taking a shower can feel like this whole project. Not emotionally in some dramatic way, just… the energy required to stand up, get the towel, turn on the water, wash my hair — it feels like more than I have available. And then I get frustrated with myself because I’m like, “Why is this so hard? It’s just a shower.”\n\nSo yes, I do feel slower. My reactions, my movements, my ability to get started — all of it feels delayed, like my body is lagging behind what my mind is telling it to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense irritability since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, definitely, though it’s hard to separate it from everything else because before I got to this heavy crash, I was kind of all over the place emotionally.\n\nThere were times when I felt almost strangely wired, like everything was finally clicking and I could see exactly what needed to happen, and I was talking faster and pushing things forward because it all felt so obvious to me. And then if someone in a meeting slowed it down or questioned a detail, I would get irritated really fast. Not just mildly annoyed — I’d snap. Over things that, normally, I think I would have just discussed or let go.\n\nLike someone would make a small comment on a design approach or ask for clarification, and I’d hear it as them being obstructive or missing the point, and I’d respond sharply. Then later I’d think, “Why did I come at them like that?” because the comment itself wasn’t that big of a deal. It was like I had no buffer. Everything felt urgent, and anyone not moving at the same pace felt like they were in the way.\n\nSo yes, intense irritability happened. It wasn’t constant every second, but when it came up it was much stronger than my usual reaction, and it came out at people in meetings over really small things.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any activities that you used to enjoy before this heavy feeling started that you have not done at all since it began?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / civil_engineer / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I can point to something I’ve done absolutely none of, if that makes sense. I mean, there are things I’ve done less, or things that feel harder to start, but “not at all” is harder for me to answer cleanly.

I don’t know if I’ve been keeping track very well, honestly. A lot of it just blurs together, and then I start questioning whether I really stopped doing something or whether I just did it once and it barely counted. So… I don’t want to overstate it.

There are definitely things that don’t feel the same or that I don’t reach for as naturally, but I’m not sure there’s a specific activity I used to enjoy that I can confidently say I have not done at all since this started.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just… I can’t shake this heavy feeling. It’s like this hopelessness has settled over everything, and I keep trying to tell myself to get up, to handle things, to be practical about it, but I can’t seem to move out from under it.\n\nAfter the divorce, I really tried to rebuild my life, you know? I thought I was putting pieces back together—work, routines, just trying to make something solid again—and now it feels like all of that has collapsed. Like everything I built is just sitting on top of me, and I’m stuck underneath it. I don’t really know what else to do, so… that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this heavy, hopeless feeling?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It didn’t happen all at once, exactly, but it started really settling in around when the financial mess after the divorce became… unavoidable, I guess. Like the paperwork and bills and all the practical things I thought I could manage started piling up, and then this heaviness just kind of came down over everything.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was just stress, that anyone would feel overwhelmed, and maybe if I got organized or made a plan it would ease up. But it’s been hanging over me for weeks now—about a month—and it hasn’t really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that. I mean, I know I’m not myself, but when you say “enjoying things,” it’s hard for me to separate what I actually don’t enjoy anymore from what I just haven’t really had the energy or space to even think about.\n\nBefore all this, I had things that felt normal or comforting, but lately everything is sort of filtered through this heaviness, if that makes sense. I’ll try to do something familiar and I’m half there, half thinking about everything that’s wrong, so it’s difficult to tell whether the thing itself doesn’t feel good anymore or whether I’m just… buried under everything.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed clear enjoyment the way I used to, but I also don’t know if I can give you a clean yes or no. Everything just feels kind of muted right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can point to anything and say, yes, that still gives me real pleasure. That’s part of what feels so unsettling. I’ll do things sometimes because I know they’re things I used to like, or because they’re supposed to help, or because I’m trying not to just sit there with the heaviness, but it doesn’t really land the same way.\n\nIt’s more like… I might have a moment where something is distracting, maybe, or familiar, but I wouldn’t call it pleasure exactly. It’s like the volume has been turned way down on everything. And then I start questioning myself—am I not enjoying it, or am I just so preoccupied that I can’t access that feeling? I’m not sure. I haven’t noticed anything recently that really cuts through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about harming yourself or wishing you weren't alive since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes. I… I have. It’s hard to say out loud, because I don’t think of myself as someone who would say that, but since this started I’ve had thoughts like… it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Not exactly like I’m calmly deciding something, more like I’m so tired and everything feels so impossible that not having to face another day starts to sound like relief.\n\nAnd I tried to tell myself it was just a passing thought, like people say things when they’re overwhelmed, but it got stronger than that. There was a point where it scared me because it didn’t feel like just some distant, dramatic thought anymore—it felt too close, like I couldn’t trust where my mind was going. So I called the employee assistance program through work. I didn’t really know what else to do, and I was embarrassed, honestly, but I scared myself enough that I made the call.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been tracking it closely, and I don’t think I’ve noticed some obvious dramatic change where my clothes suddenly fit completely differently or anything like that.\n\nIt’s possible there’s been some change, but I can’t confidently say. I’ve been so focused on just getting through the day that I haven’t been paying much attention to the scale or even to my body in that way. I guess if you’re asking whether I’ve clearly lost or gained a lot, I don’t think I can say that I have. I just don’t know for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Sleep has been… really bad. It’s not just that I’m going to bed late or something normal like that. I’ll be exhausted, like my body feels heavy and I know I should be able to sleep, but then I just lie there staring at the ceiling.\n\nMy mind starts replaying everything — bills, things I’m behind on, mistakes I might have made at work, things I said wrong or forgot to do — and it just loops. I’ll think I’ve settled down, and then another thought comes in and I’m wide awake again.\n\nAnd then other nights, or sometimes the same night, I’ll wake up before dawn and it feels like I never really slept at all. Like I must have been asleep at some point, but I don’t feel rested. I just wake up already tense and tired, and then I’m stuck there waiting for the day to start.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. Everything feels slowed down, like I’m trying to move through something thick. Getting through a normal workday feels like dragging wet concrete. I know that sounds dramatic, but that’s honestly the closest way I can describe it.\n\nEven things that should be automatic feel like they require this huge negotiation with myself. Taking a shower, getting dressed, opening my laptop… it’s like I look at the thing and I understand what needs to happen, but my body just doesn’t want to start. Or it starts, but every step feels heavier than it should.\n\nAt work especially, I’ll sit there knowing I need to answer an email or pull up a file, and it feels almost ridiculous how much effort it takes just to begin. It’s not that I don’t care. I do care, and that almost makes it worse, because I can see the work piling up and I’m just… not able to move through it the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes, but it’s a little confusing because at the same time I feel slowed down and heavy. It’s like physically I’m exhausted, but I also can’t just sit still with it.\n\nI’ve been pacing around my apartment a lot, especially when there’s nothing useful left to do. I’ll walk from the kitchen to the living room and back again, checking my phone, opening my laptop, closing it, standing up again. It doesn’t even feel productive half the time, just this internal pressure like I should be doing something.\n\nAnd at work, or around work, I started pushing myself into extra things — like extra proposals, calling colleagues because I suddenly had another idea, volunteering for tasks that no one actually asked me to take on. I’d tell myself, “If I just take care of this one more thing, maybe I’ll feel less awful,” but then it would turn into five things.\n\nSo yes, restless is probably the right word. Not always fidgety in a small way, but more like I can’t settle, and I keep trying to force myself into activity even when I don’t have the energy for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about things that wouldn't normally bother you since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes. Constantly. And it’s not even proportional to what actually happened, I know that somewhere in my head, but it doesn’t feel that way.\n\nI keep telling myself I’m a terrible engineer. Like if I miss something small, or I have to double-check a calculation, or someone sends back a comment on a plan, my brain turns it into this whole indictment of me as a person. I’ll replay it over and over, like, “How could you miss that? What if that caused a bigger problem? What if everyone knows you’re barely holding it together?” Even tense emails — not even openly hostile ones, just slightly curt or corrective — I’ll read them again and again and think, “See, you’re ruining things. You’re making everyone else’s job harder.”\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I keep thinking I’m a failed wife, like I should be better, warmer, more present, less exhausting to be around. I feel like a burden. Like people have to manage me, or make allowances for me, or tiptoe around me, and then I feel guilty for that too.\n\nIt’s like every little mistake becomes proof of some bigger failure. A missed calculation, a delayed response, a tense conversation — I collect them in my head and use them against myself. I don’t want to, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving or reacting more slowly than usual since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, it feels less like I’m choosing to move slowly and more like everything takes this unreasonable amount of effort now.\n\nGetting through a normal workday feels like dragging wet concrete. I’ll know exactly what I need to do, but getting myself to actually do it physically — opening the laptop, starting a task, even just shifting from one thing to the next — feels like I’m trying to push through something heavy. Not emotionally in some dramatic way, just… the energy required to stand up, get the towel, turn on the water, wash my hair — it feels like more than I have available. And then I get frustrated with myself because I’m like, “Why is this so hard? It’s just a shower.”\n\nSo yes, I do feel slower. My reactions, my movements, my ability to get started — all of it feels delayed, like my body is lagging behind what my mind is telling it to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense irritability since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: Yes. And then if someone in a meeting slowed it down or questioned a detail, I would get irritated really fast. Not just mildly annoyed — I’d snap. So… I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nThere are definitely things that don’t feel the same or that I don’t reach for as naturally, but I’m not sure there’s a specific activity I used to enjoy that I can confidently say I have not done at all since this started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m… not totally sure how to answer that cleanly.

I don’t know if it’s exactly that I’ve lost interest, like the interest has disappeared, or if everything just feels so hard to access right now that it looks the same from the outside. Because there are things I know I normally like — or at least I remember liking them — but when I think about actually doing them, it feels like there’s this wall between me and the activity.

So I guess I’d say I haven’t felt drawn to things the way I usually do. But I’m hesitant to say, “Yes, I’ve lost interest,” as if I feel nothing about them, because sometimes I’ll think, “I should want to do that,” or “That used to help,” and there’s still some part of me that knows it matters. It’s just not pulling me in.

It’s more like the spark is muted. Or like the idea of enjoying something feels theoretical. I can recognize that something is supposed to be enjoyable, but I don’t feel naturally motivated toward it. And then I get stuck trying to figure out whether that counts as loss of interest or just being too weighed down to follow through.

So… yes, maybe somewhat, but I’m not confident labeling it. I’ve noticed things don’t feel as appealing or easy to engage with, but I can’t give you a neat answer beyond that.

Everything felt urgent, and anyone not moving at the same pace felt like they were in the way.\n\nSo yes, intense irritability happened. It wasn’t constant every second, but when it came up it was much stronger than my usual reaction, and it came out at people in meetings over really small things.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you used to enjoy before this heavy feeling started that you have not done at all since it began?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to something I’ve done absolutely none of, if that makes sense. I mean, there are things I’ve done less, or things that feel harder to start, but “not at all” is harder for me to answer cleanly.\n\nI don’t know if I’ve been keeping track very well, honestly. A lot of it just blurs together, and then I start questioning whether I really stopped doing something or whether I just did it once and it barely counted. So… I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nThere are definitely things that don’t feel the same or that I don’t reach for as naturally, but I’m not sure there’s a specific activity I used to enjoy that I can confidently say I have not done at all since this started.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI don’t know if it’s exactly that I’ve lost interest, like the interest has disappeared, or if everything just feels so hard to access right now that it looks the same from the outside. Because there are things I know I normally like — or at least I remember liking them — but when I think about actually doing them, it feels like there’s this wall between me and the activity.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t felt drawn to things the way I usually do. But I’m hesitant to say, “Yes, I’ve lost interest,” as if I feel nothing about them, because sometimes I’ll think, “I should want to do that,” or “That used to help,” and there’s still some part of me that knows it matters. It’s just not pulling me in.\n\nIt’s more like the spark is muted. Or like the idea of enjoying something feels theoretical. I can recognize that something is supposed to be enjoyable, but I don’t feel naturally motivated toward it. And then I get stuck trying to figure out whether that counts as loss of interest or just being too weighed down to follow through.\n\nSo… yes, maybe somewhat, but I’m not confident labeling it. So… I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nThere are definitely things that don’t feel the same or that I don’t reach for as naturally, but I’m not sure there’s a specific activity I used to enjoy that I can confidently say I have not done at all since this started.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy since this heavy feeling started?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI don’t know if it’s exactly that I’ve lost interest, like the interest has disappeared, or if everything just feels so hard to access right now that it looks the same from the outside. Because there are things I know I normally like — or at least I remember liking them — but when I think about actually doing them, it feels like there’s this wall between me and the activity.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t felt drawn to things the way I usually do. But I’m hesitant to say, “Yes, I’ve lost interest,” as if I feel nothing about them, because sometimes I’ll think, “I should want to do that,” or “That used to help,” and there’s still some part of me that knows it matters. It’s just not pulling me in.\n\nIt’s more like the spark is muted. Or like the idea of enjoying something feels theoretical. I can recognize that something is supposed to be enjoyable, but I don’t feel naturally motivated toward it. And then I get stuck trying to figure out whether that counts as loss of interest or just being too weighed down to follow through.\n\nSo… yes, maybe somewhat, but I’m not confident labeling it. I’ve noticed things don’t feel as appealing or easy to engage with, but I can’t give you a neat answer beyond that.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling worthless since this heavy feeling started?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / civil_engineer / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Worthlessness_Guilt\nmanifestation: \"I keep telling myself I’m a terrible engineer, a failed wife, and a burden, and I replay every missed calculation or tense email like proof that I ruin things.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Very much, actually.

It’s not just feeling bad about myself in a general way. It’s like my brain keeps building a case against me, over and over. I’ll be sitting there and suddenly I’m telling myself I’m a terrible engineer, like I shouldn’t be trusted with anything important, and then I start replaying every missed calculation or every tense email or every time I didn’t explain something clearly enough as if it proves I ruin things.

And it doesn’t stay only at work. It spills into everything. I keep thinking I’m a failed wife, like I’m not showing up the way I’m supposed to, like being around me is just exhausting or disappointing. Then that turns into this thought that I’m a burden — at home, at work, everywhere. Like people would have an easier time if they didn’t have to deal with me.

I know, on some level, that one mistake or one awkward email shouldn’t mean all of that. If someone else said this about themselves, I’d probably tell them they were being unfair. But when it’s me, it feels convincing. I just keep going back through things, picking out evidence that I’m failing, and it’s hard to stop once it starts.
